# Desloppify First-Time User Experience: Structured Feedback Report

## Execution Summary

I ran the full workflow (`scan` -> `status` -> `show abstraction_fitness` -> `show concerns` -> `next --count 3 --explain` -> `plan`) against the desloppify codebase itself (641 files, 126K LOC). The overall score was 90.6/100.

---

## Rating 1: End-to-End Flow (scan -> status -> show -> next -> plan)

**Rating: 8/10**

**Positives:**
- The scan output ends with explicit next-step guidance: `Run 'desloppify next' for the highest-priority item.` and `Run 'desloppify status' for the full dashboard.` This is exactly what a new user needs.
- The status command builds on the scan output well, giving a tabular dimension-by-dimension breakdown with tier assignments and action types.
- Every command references other commands, so you never feel lost about what to do next.
- The scan footer includes a "Score recipe" section explaining how the overall score is composed, plus "Biggest weighted drags" which immediately tells you where to focus.

**Negatives:**
- The scan output is dense. The step-by-step detector progress (`[1/15] Unused (ruff)... [2/15] Structural analysis...`) is fine for power users but could overwhelm a first-timer before they reach the meaningful summary.
- The status command outputs a LOT: dimension table, structural debt by area, an "AGENT PLAN" section, workflow instructions, and multiple coaching lines. It feels like three commands crammed into one. A first-time user would struggle to parse what matters.
- The transition from `status` to `show` is not obvious -- status tells me to run `desloppify show <area>` in one place and `desloppify show <detector>` in another. These are different concepts (area = directory path, detector = finding type) but the distinction is never explained.

**Key quote (good):**
```
Score plateaued at 90.6 for 5 scans. Abstraction fit (48.0%) is where the breakthrough is.
```
This is excellent coaching -- it tells me the score is stuck and where to invest.

**Key quote (confusing):**
```
open (in-scope): 743 · open (out-of-scope carried): 3680 · open (global): 4423
```
What are "out-of-scope carried" and "global"? These are never explained. A first-time user sees 743 findings in the scan but 4423 in the status and has no idea why.

---

## Rating 2: Subjective Dimension Access (`show abstraction_fitness`)

**Rating: 3/10**

**This was largely a dead end.** The output was:

```
Abstraction fit: 48.0% health (strict: 48.0%)
'abstraction_fitness' is a subjective dimension — its score comes from design reviews, not code findings.
4 open review finding(s). Run `show review --status open`.
Subjective quality (<98%): Abstraction fit 48.0%, Contracts 65.0%, Error consistency 65.3%
Next command to improve subjective scores: `desloppify review --run-batches --runner codex --parallel --scan-after-import --dimensions abstraction_fitness,contract_coherence,error_consistency`
```

That is the entire output. Five lines. For a dimension at 48.0% -- the single biggest drag on the overall score -- I expected to see:
- What files are pulling this score down
- What specific issues were found
- What "abstraction fitness" even means in practice

Instead, I got a redirect to `show review --status open` with no additional context. The suggested command to fix it (`review --run-batches --runner codex --parallel --scan-after-import --dimensions ...`) requires an external AI runner ("codex") that a first-time user likely does not have configured. There is no fallback path offered.

The score IS shown (48.0%), which is good. But there is no explanation of WHY it is 48.0%, no drill-down, and no human-actionable remediation. The only path forward involves running an AI batch review process.

---

## Rating 3: Score Guide Visibility

**Rating: 7/10**

The score guide appears in the `status` output:
```
Score guide: overall (lenient) · objective (mechanical only) · strict (penalizes wontfix) · verified (scan-confirmed only)
Focus on strict — it's your north star.
```

This is concise and clear. However:
- It does NOT appear in the `scan` output. The scan just says `Scores: overall 90.6/100  objective 94.9/100  strict 90.6/100  verified 94.9/100` with no explanation. A first-time user running only `scan` would see four scores and have no idea what they mean or which to care about.
- The one-word descriptions ("lenient", "mechanical only", "penalizes wontfix", "scan-confirmed only") are adequate but could be more helpful. "Penalizes wontfix" requires knowing what "wontfix" status means first.
- The "Score recipe" section in scan output is excellent: `overall = 40% mechanical + 60% subjective` with pool averages and biggest weighted drags. This partially compensates for the missing guide.

---

## Rating 4: Explain Quality (`next --explain`)

**Rating: 4/10**

The `--explain` flag added this line to each item:
```
explain: ranked by tier=1, confidence=high, count=0, id=review::.::holistic::abstraction_fitness::typed_dict_contract_softening::5e16e1c8
```

This is sort keys, not strategic context. It tells me the item is Tier 1 with high confidence and a count of 0. It does NOT tell me:
- Why this item matters more than others (e.g., "this impacts the Abstraction fit dimension which is at 48.0%, the biggest drag on your overall score")
- What the estimated score impact would be if resolved
- Which dimension this finding belongs to (the dimension IS embedded in the ID string `::abstraction_fitness::` but it is not called out explicitly in the explain line)
- How this relates to the user's goal of reaching 95

The finding descriptions themselves are quite good:
```
TypedDict-backed payload/state boundaries are softened by `.get()` + default coercion, masking schema drift and invalid runtime shapes.
```
This is specific, technical, and actionable. The suggestion is also solid:
```
Suggestion: Introduce strict boundary constructors for `ReviewImportPayload` and `StateModel` slices...
```

But the `--explain` flag specifically adds very little value beyond what was already shown. It is essentially a debug dump of sort keys.

The "Tier Navigator" at the top is helpful:
```
Tier Navigator
  Open: T1:6 T2:93 T3:410 T4:249
  Switch: `desloppify next --tier 1` | `desloppify next --tier 2` ...
```

And the footer provides good strategic framing:
```
North star: strict 90.6/100 -> target 98.0 (+7.4 needed)
```

---

## Rating 5: Plan Readability

**Rating: 6/10**

**Positives:**
- The plan is structured as Markdown with checkbox items -- copy-paste friendly for task tracking.
- The dimension health table at the top is a clean overview.
- Tier groupings are clear: `Tier 1: Highest priority (auto-fixable + high-confidence holistic review) (6 open)`.
- Items are grouped by file, which makes batch-fixing natural.
- T1 descriptions are accurate and actionable. The 6 T1 items include 5 holistic review findings and 1 unused import -- all legitimate high-priority items.

**Negatives:**
- The plan is 3,129 lines long. This is not a "plan" -- it is a complete inventory of every finding in the codebase. A plan should be a curated subset with sequencing and estimated impact. Dumping 743 findings into a Markdown document is not actionable.
- T4 is dominated by `subjective_review::...::unreviewed` entries -- literally hundreds of lines saying "No design review on record -- run `desloppify review --prepare`". These are all the same action (run the review) but listed individually per file, creating massive noise.
- The footer is weak: `AGENT PLAN: 1. Start from the top-ranked action in this plan. Next command: 'desloppify next --count 20'`. After 3,129 lines of output, sending the user back to `next` feels like a loop.
- There is no estimated score impact per tier. If I fix all 6 T1 items, what does my score become? If I address T2, how much does the needle move? Without this, I cannot prioritize my time.

**Key quote (T1 accuracy):**
```
Tier 1 (Highest priority (auto-fixable + high-confidence holistic review)): 6 open / 172 total (97% addressed)
```
The T1 content is accurate: one genuine unused import auto-fix, plus 5 high-confidence holistic review findings. The tier description says "auto-fixable + high-confidence holistic review" which matches. The parenthetical "(97% addressed)" is a nice touch showing progress.

---

## Rating 6: Dead Ends Encountered

**Rating: 6/10 (approximately 2 significant dead ends)**

**Dead end 1: `show abstraction_fitness`** -- Gave a 5-line redirect with no actionable content. The lowest-scoring dimension in the codebase (48.0%) produced less useful output than any other command. The only remediation path requires an external AI runner.

**Dead end 2: The `--explain` flag** -- Promised strategic context but delivered sort keys. A user adding `--explain` is asking "why should I do this first?" and gets back `tier=1, confidence=high, count=0` which is the same information already shown by the tier/confidence labels on the finding itself.

**Near-dead-end: `show concerns`** -- Produced 127 entries, many of which are "systemic smell" roll-ups. While individually informative, the output has no summary, no prioritization, and no obvious exit action. After scrolling through 127 entries, there is no "here is what to do first" footer. It just ends.

---

## Rating 7: Overall First-Time Experience

**Rating: 7/10**

**What works well:**
- The scan-to-status-to-next pipeline is smooth and each step references the next
- The "Score recipe" and "Biggest weighted drags" sections are genuinely insightful coaching
- The `next` command delivers focused, actionable items with good descriptions and suggestions
- The tier system (T1-T4) makes intuitive sense with good labels
- The dimensional health bars are visually effective
- The "North star" framing in `next` output is motivating
- The "Score plateaued at 90.6 for 5 scans" observation is remarkably useful intelligence

**What needs improvement:**
- Subjective dimensions are a black box with no drill-down path for users without an AI review runner
- The plan is too long to be useful as a plan -- it is an inventory, not a strategy
- The `--explain` flag does not deliver on its implied promise
- The distinction between "area" and "detector" in `show` is never explained
- The "out-of-scope carried" / "global" counts in status are unexplained and confusing
- There is no score projection anywhere: "If you fix T1+T2, your score goes from 90.6 to ~X"

**As a first-time user trying to reach 95:**
I would know to focus on Abstraction fit (48.0%) and Contracts (65.0%) from the scan coaching. But I would immediately hit a wall because improving these requires the AI review system, and no human-actionable alternative is offered. For the mechanical dimensions, the path is clear: T1 has 6 items, T2 has 93 items, and `next` delivers them one at a time. But the mechanical pool is already at 94.9%, so fixing mechanical issues alone cannot reach 95 overall (since overall = 40% mechanical + 60% subjective). The tool correctly identifies the subjective dimensions as the bottleneck but does not give a workable path for a user without Codex or another batch runner.

---

## Summary Table

| Criterion | Rating | Key Issue |
|-----------|--------|-----------|
| End-to-end flow | 8/10 | Smooth pipeline, but status is overloaded |
| Subjective dimension access | 3/10 | Dead end -- no drill-down, AI runner required |
| Score guide visibility | 7/10 | Present in status, absent from scan |
| Explain quality | 4/10 | Sort keys, not strategic context |
| Plan readability | 6/10 | 3,129 lines is inventory, not plan |
| Dead ends encountered | 6/10 | Two significant dead ends |
| Overall first-time experience | 7/10 | Good coaching, but subjective dims are a wall |

**Overall average: 5.9/10**

The tool's strongest feature is its coaching intelligence -- the "plateaued at 90.6", "biggest weighted drags", and "score recipe" sections are genuinely excellent. Its weakest area is the subjective dimension experience, where the tool identifies the problem (Abstraction fit at 48%) but provides no human-accessible remediation path. The `plan` command also needs a "summary plan" mode that shows the top 20 items with projected score impact, rather than a 3,000-line exhaustive listing.

# Feedback Report: Navigating the `naming_quality` Dimension in Desloppify

## What I Learned

The `naming_quality` dimension is a **subjective dimension** scored at **90.0%** (strict: 90.0%). It comes from an AI-powered holistic design review, not from mechanical code detectors. The dimension looks for: generic verbs that reveal nothing (process, handle, do), name/behavior mismatches (getX() that mutates state), vocabulary divergence from codebase norms, and inconsistent abbreviations.

There is also a mechanical `naming` **detector** (separate from the dimension) that found 6 directory-level naming inconsistencies (mixed snake_case/flat_lower file naming), but this detector's output does **not** feed into the naming_quality dimension score.

The score itself is a single number (90.0) stored in `subjective_assessments` from a holistic review done at `2026-02-26T11:00:58+00:00`, with no breakdown of what files or functions contributed to the 10% penalty.

## Command-by-Command Experience

### 1. `scan` -- Good starting point
Found the naming_quality score in the dimension table (90.0%). The scan output is dense but the scorecard section is clear. The "biggest weighted drags" section helpfully told me naming_quality is NOT among the worst offenders, providing useful context even though it wasn't what I was looking for.

### 2. `show naming_quality` -- Dead end
This was my biggest frustration. The output was:

> ```
> Naming quality: 90.0% health (strict: 90.0%)
> 'naming_quality' is a subjective dimension -- its score comes from design reviews, not code findings.
> Subjective quality (<98%): Abstraction fit 48.0%, Contracts 65.0%, Error consistency 65.3%
> Next command to improve subjective scores: `desloppify review --run-batches --runner codex...`
> ```

I asked to see naming_quality, and the tool redirected me to other dimensions (Abstraction fit, Contracts, Error consistency). It did not show me:
- What naming problems exist
- Which files have naming issues
- What the 10% deduction came from
- How the 90% score was calculated

The suggested next command targets `abstraction_fitness,contract_coherence,error_consistency` -- not `naming_quality`. The tool literally ignored my question and told me to work on something else.

### 3. `next --explain` -- Useful but unrelated
The default `next` command showed the global highest-priority item (about abstraction_fitness). The `--explain` flag added only one line of sort metadata: `explain: ranked by tier=1, confidence=high, count=0`. That's not strategic explanation -- it's sort key debugging.

I had to discover `--scope naming_quality` on my own, which gave slightly better output but still just echoed the score and suggested `desloppify review --prepare --dimensions naming_quality`.

### 4. `plan` -- Comprehensive but naming_quality is a needle in a haystack
The 159KB output included naming_quality in the dimension table (showing 0 issues, T4), and deep in the T4 section, one line: `[subjective] Subjective dimension below target: Naming quality (90.0%)` with the review prepare action. Useful if you read the entire plan, but no way to zoom in.

### 5. `detect naming` -- The hidden gem
Running `detect naming` directly gave the most concrete output: 6 directories with mixed file naming conventions, showing majority/minority style and specific outlier files. But this information is completely disconnected from the naming_quality dimension score (90.0% comes from an AI review, not this detector). The naming detector findings also don't appear in `show naming` (returns "No open findings matching: naming").

---

## Ratings

### 1. Score Discoverability: 7/10
The score (90.0%) was easy to find in `scan`, `status`, and `plan` output. The dimension table format is clear and the bar chart visualization helps. Lost points because: `show naming_quality` should have shown the score more prominently with its context (weight in overall score, historical trend), and the `naming` detector findings being invisible in `show` is confusing.

### 2. Actionability: 3/10
This is the biggest weakness. The tool never told me WHAT specific naming problems exist. Every path led to "run an AI review." Specifically:
- `show naming_quality` said it's subjective, go fix other things.
- `next --scope naming_quality` said "run `desloppify review --prepare --dimensions naming_quality`."
- `review --prepare` produced a batch of 80 files to review, but no indication of what naming issues to look for.
- The `detect naming` command found concrete issues (6 directories with mixed conventions), but those are disconnected from the score.

A user who wants to improve naming_quality today has no actionable list of "fix these specific things."

### 3. Score Model Clarity: 5/10
The `status` command helpfully included: `Score guide: overall (lenient) . objective (mechanical only) . strict (penalizes wontfix) . verified (scan-confirmed only)`. That one line is excellent. But there are still confusing aspects:
- The naming_quality row shows `Checks: --` and `Issues: 0`, yet the score is 90.0%. If there are 0 issues and no checks, where does the 10% penalty come from?
- Digging into the state JSON, I found `checks: 10, issues: 0, weighted_failures: 1.0, assessment_score: 90.0` -- so there ARE 10 checks with a 90% pass rate, but this is never exposed in any CLI output.
- The relationship between "assessment_score" and the actual per-file naming problems is completely opaque.

### 4. Explain Usefulness: 2/10
The `--explain` flag on `next` added this single line: `explain: ranked by tier=1, confidence=high, count=0, id=...`. When scoped to naming_quality: `explain: Subjective dimensions are always queued as T4 and do not outrank mechanical T1/T2/T3 items.`

The second version is actually useful -- it explains why naming_quality is deprioritized. But neither gives strategic context about naming_quality itself (what it measures, why it matters, what improving it would do to the overall score). The flag name "explain" sets an expectation of explanation that isn't met.

### 5. Subjective Dimension Handling: 3/10
`show naming_quality` was essentially a dead end. It confirmed the score and then immediately pivoted to other dimensions with lower scores. It did not:
- Show the definition of naming_quality (which I had to find by digging into query.json's `dimension_prompts`)
- List any specific naming issues found during previous reviews
- Show which files were reviewed or which got flagged
- Connect to the `detect naming` mechanical detector

The tool treats subjective dimensions as black boxes with just a number. The `dimension_prompts` data in query.json (quoted above) is excellent -- it lists exactly what to look for and what to skip. This should be surfaced in `show naming_quality`.

### 6. Overall Navigability: 4/10
My journey was:
1. `scan` -- found the score quickly. Good.
2. `show naming_quality` -- dead end, redirected to other dimensions.
3. Tried `show naming` -- "No open findings." Confusing because `detect naming` finds things.
4. `next --scope naming_quality` -- just echoed the score and said to run a review.
5. `plan` -- buried in 159KB output.
6. `review --prepare --dimensions naming_quality` -- prepared a batch but still no concrete issues.
7. `detect naming` -- finally found concrete naming inconsistencies, but these are disconnected from the score.
8. Dug into JSON state files -- found the actual score breakdown.

The tool makes it easy to see that naming_quality is 90%, but makes it hard to understand WHY or what to DO. For mechanical dimensions (like code_quality or file_health), I suspect the experience is much better because `show <detector>` would list specific findings with files and descriptions. For subjective dimensions, the user hits a wall.

---

## Key Recommendations (as a user)

1. **`show naming_quality` should show the dimension definition** -- the "look_for" and "skip" criteria from the dimension_prompts are already in the system. Surface them.

2. **`show naming_quality` should show related findings** -- even if there are 0 open review findings for naming_quality, show the `detect naming` results as "related mechanical signals." The disconnect between the naming detector and the naming_quality dimension is confusing.

3. **`show naming_quality` should NOT redirect to other dimensions** -- when I ask about naming_quality, showing "Abstraction fit 48.0%, Contracts 65.0%" is misdirecting. Show me naming_quality content or say "no detailed findings available, run a review to discover issues."

4. **`--explain` should explain the dimension, not just the sort order** -- include what the dimension measures, its weight in the overall score, and what score improvement would mean in overall terms.

5. **Surface the score decomposition** -- the state file has `checks: 10, assessment_score: 90.0, weighted_failures: 1.0`, but the CLI shows `Checks: --, Issues: 0`. The raw data is more informative than the display.
